Enriched bi(co)ends
Nicola Carissimi

Abstract
In this paper we introduce the theory of ends and coends in the context of enriched bicategories. This will be an enriched version of the theory introduced in [ Cor16 ] , and a bicategorical version of the classical theory of enriched (co)ends, which can be found in [ Kel05 ] or in the more recent [ Lor21 ] . One of the main obstacles to the construction of such a theory is the amount of structure involved at this stage of categorification. A great help will be furnished by strictification results (Section 2 ), as well as the powerful tool of string diagrams (Section 3 ), essential for making calculations manageable by a human being.



1
Introduction

Bicategories enriched over a monoidal bicategory
\mathcal{V}
were first introduced by
[
GS16
]
, and the present paper can also be considered as a continuation of their work. In that paper, the theory of enriched bicategories is wisely developed avoiding recourse to any kind of symmetry on the base of the enrichment, such as a braiding, which is necessary for the elementary constructions that will be presented here. These include the opposite
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory (Section
4.2
) and the tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories (Section
4.3
). These constructions, together with the notion of closed monoidal bicategory and its self-enrichment (Section
4.4
), allow us to introduce the theory of enriched bi(co)ends (Section
6
), opening the way to the definition of a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory of
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors (
6.2
), among the other things.

Two main results presented in this article are then the (verification of the) definition of opposite and tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories (Theorem
4.15
and Theorem
4.20
), when
\mathcal{V}
is braided. Also, it is proved that a right closed monoidal bicategory is self-enriched (Theorem
4.24
), and under the hypothesis of being closed, the theory of enriched biends and bicoends is introduced. In order to introduce this theory we define the enriched bicategorical version of extra-natural transformations (Section
5
, and construction of a class of examples
5.1
). These (enriched)
extra-pseudonatural transformations
form a category, and we prove that enriched bi(co)ends are representing objects for this category (Proposition
6.4
), providing a conceptual clarification of the axioms defining them. This clarification appears to be new even with respect to the plain, non-enriched context of
[
Cor16
]
.

Acknowledgments:

This article contains some of the results of my PhD thesis, written at the University of Lille under the supervision and with the kind help of Ivo Dell’Ambrogio, and with the financial support of Labex CEMPI and the University of Lille. I am thankful to colleagues and friends for the fruitful exchanges.

2
Strictification results

In this section we recollect the available strictification results for monoidal and braided monoidal bicategories, specializing more general results on tricategories. The theory of strictification for monoidal bicategories arises from the fact that tricategories cannot be fully stratified, not even in their one-object case (monoidal bicategories), nor in their one-object and one morphism case (braided monoidal categories). The standard reference for this is
[
GPS95
]
, establishing that

Any tricategory is triequivalent to a Gray category.

This theorem boils down to its one-object case:

Theorem 2.1

(Gordon–Power–Street)
.

Any monoidal bicategory is monoidally biequivalent to a Gray monoid.

As one starts to consider a braided structure on such a monoidal bicategory, the theory naturally lands in a higher categorical level. Since even the definition given by Todd Trimble of a tetracategory is fairly wild, it is understandable that a fully developed theory of strictification has yet to appear. However, the one-object and one-morphism case of a tetracategory (
e.g.
a braided monoidal bicategory) and its strictification have been in fact well studied, and references considered in this paper for that are the work of Crans
[
Cra98
]
and Gurski
[
Gur11
]
. The first provided a definition of what a
semi-strict braided monoidal bicategory
is, and the second proved the following:

Theorem 2.2

(Gurski)
.

Any braided monoidal bicategory is braided monoidally biequivalent to a semi-strict braided monoidal bicategory.

What a Gray monoid (also known as a semi-strict monoidal bicategory) and a semi-strict braided monoidal bicategory are, will be briefly recalled in the next two subsections. An important aspect which is highlighted in
[
Gur11
]
is that a semi-strict braided monoidal bicategory isn’t just a semi-strict monoidal bicategory with a braided structure, but it satisfies more axioms, enhancing the power of the strictification result.

2.1
Gray monoids

The Gray tensor product
\otimes_{G}
has been first introduced in
[
Gra74
]
, and defines a symmetric monoidal structure on the category of 2-categories and 2-functors. A
Gray monoid
is then a monoid for this monoidal operation. The resulting structure happens to be a monoidal bicategory (this is checked for example at
[
Gur06
]
, Section 5, by means of
cubical pseudofunctors
), which is not a monoidal 2-category, since we replaced the cartesian structure with the Gray tensor product. Nonetheless, it’s tensor product is strictly associative and unital. The semi-strictness is due to the fact that for every two pairs of objects
(A,B)
,
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
and 1-cells
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
g\colon B\to B^{\prime}
, the commutativity of the interchange law for a monoidal 2-category is replaced by 2-isomorphisms called
interchangers
:

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\otimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}\Sigma_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}\otimes g}

Axioms for the interchanger will be given in Section
3
. References for these axioms are, for example, the more general case of those for a Gray category in
[
GPS95
]
.

2.2
Braided Gray monoids and semi-strict braided monoidal bicategories

Braided monoidal bicategory where first introduced at
[
McC00
]
. The structure (see Definition
2.5
) is given by the braiding morphism, together two 2-isomorphisms
R
and
S
indexed by triples of objects and replacing the commutative hexagons of a braided monoidal category, satisfying four axioms. In the author’s PhD thesis
[
Car24
]
a string diagrammatic non-strict formalism of the axioms for a braiding is given. Here, we are going to directly state the strict version of the same axioms (in Section
3.2
), in which the monoidal structure of the braided monoidal bicategory is assumed to be that of a Gray monoid.

Remark 2.4
.

Strictification for a braided monoidal bicategory yields a more specific structure than just a braided structure on a Gray monoid. The strictification theorem for braided monoidal bicategories would otherwise be just an easy consequence of the strictification for monoidal bicategories via a lifting of the braided structure.

The following is going to define both the structure of a braided Gray monoid and that of a semi-strict monoidal bicategory. It will be highlighted the difference between the braiding axioms, which are just a strict form of the axioms for a braided monoidal bicategory, and the additional constraints that we can achieve via a braided monoidal strictification.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\sigma_{G}
denote the symmetry for the symmetric monoidal category of 2-categories and 2-functors under the Gray tensor product. A
braided structure on a Gray monoid

\mathcal{B}
consist of a pseudonatural equivalence of 2-functors
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}\otimes_{G}\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}\otimes_{G}\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}\beta}

and two modifications, for every triple of objects
A,B,C
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes C\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes A\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes C\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}S}

satisfying the four braiding axioms (
BA1
)-(
BA4
) below in Section
3.2
.

A braided Gray monoid is, moreover, a
semi-strict braided monoidal bicategory
if it satisfied the following further axioms (see
[
Cra98
]
Definition 2.2). For every pair of objects
A,B
in
\mathcal{B}
, the following pair of squares commute:

(s1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes\mathbb{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{1}\otimes A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes\mathbb{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

and the following identities hold true:

(s2)

\hbox to233.49pt{\vbox to67.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 116.74472pt\lower-25.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

.

Observe that in axioms (
s2

s3
) every square commutes, either because of (

) (which also gives commutative squares in (

)), or because of strictness of unitality, like
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces AB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces AB\mathbb{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BA\mathbb{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta 1}
; or again because of unitality for the pseudonatural transformation
\beta
, which evaluated at the identical monoidal unitor gives the commutativity of, for example,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces AB\mathbb{1}}

3
String diagrams

In this section we fix notations for string diagrams and we point out the main rules that will be subsequently used. For what concerns the bicategorical structure, the orientation of 1-cell will be from the bottom to the top, while that of 2-cells is from left to right. For example, a 2-cell
\alpha\colon f\circ g\circ h\Rightarrow k\circ\ell
is given by
\alpha
f
g
h
\ell
.
k

Each region is labeled with an object which is the domain of the string above it and the codomain of the string below it. These objects will usually remain implicit.

Remark 3.1
.

The usual rules making string diagrams a powerful language and which are used on a constant base in calculations are the Eckmann-Hilton argument for 2-cells
\alpha\colon f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
and
\beta\colon g\Rightarrow g^{\prime}
:
\alpha
\beta
\alpha
\beta
f^{\prime}
g^{\prime}
f
g
=
f
g
f^{\prime}
g^{\prime}

and the triangular identities for internal adjunctions
f\dashv g
:
=
g
g
g
g
f

and

=
f
f
f.
f
g

Remark 3.2
.

In the case of the bicategory of pseudofunctors between two fixed bicategories, pseudonatural transformations and modifications, a source of rules for string calculus comes from the axioms for a pseudonatural transformation (naturality in particular) and from the modification axiom. Translated in diagrams, they say the following:

•

Let
t\colon F\Rightarrow G
a pseudonatural transformation of pseudofunctors. That means, for every
u\colon X\to Y
there are invertible crossings (2-cells)
t_{Y}
Fu.
Gu
t_{X}

Then, the naturality axiom says that for every 2-cell
\alpha\colon u\to u^{\prime}
in the domain bicategory we have

(3.3)

Gu

•

If
M\colon t\Rrightarrow s
is a modification, then the data of the family of 2-cells

is subject to the equality

(3.4)

s_{Y}

for every

3.1
Strings for monoidal bicategories

When we deal with a monoidal bicategory, we will always interpret juxtaposition of (1- and 2-) morphisms in a string diagram as a tensor product. Juxtaposition with 1 is tensoring with the identity morphism. For example, if
f,g\colon A\to B
are 1-cells and
\alpha\colon f\Rightarrow g
, the 2-cell
\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\alpha\colon C\otimes f\Rightarrow C\otimes g
will be denoted
1\alpha
1f
1g

and if
\beta\colon h\Rightarrow k
is another 2-cell, the tensor product
\alpha\otimes\beta
is denoted
\alpha\beta
fh
gk.

Remark 3.5
.

It is here important to notice that to talk about a tensored pair of morphisms
fg
is an abuse of notation consisting of identifying the two composites
1g\circ f1
or
f1\circ 1g
. This is fundamentally permitted by the role of the interchanger, since the interchanger axioms allow the usual calculus rule justifying the omissions in computations. The axioms for the interchanger (
2.3
), which we will make a heavy and sometimes implicit use of, especially in the proof of Theorem
4.20
, are as follows:

(
i
)

\Sigma_{\mathrm{id},g}=\mathrm{id}_{g},\hskip 20.00003pt\Sigma_{f,\mathrm{id}}=\mathrm{id}_{f}.

(
ii
)

1\Sigma_{f,g}=\Sigma_{1f,g},\hskip 20.00003pt\Sigma_{f1,g}=\Sigma_{f,1g},\hskip 20.00003pt\Sigma_{f,g}1=\Sigma_{f,g1}.

(
iii
)

Remark 3.6
.

Since the braided monoidal bicategories we work with are always assumed to be semi-strict, we have the following equality which also will be repeatedly used in the proof of Theorem
4.20
. We may refer to this operation as a “joining of identities”, and it just says that for any
f,g\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and any 2-cell

3.2

3.3
Strings and pseudoadjunctions

3.3.1
Pseudoadjunctions

A coherent notion of adjunction of pseudofunctors between bicategories (or 1-cells in a possibly weak tricategory) is that of a
pseudoadjunction
. A more general notion has been first introduced in
[
BP88
]
, and is there called
local adjunction
, but is probably nowadays more commonly known as
lax 2-adjunction
. Let us recall these definitions and fix some notations. Two pseudofunctors
F\colon\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\xrightarrow{}\\[-2.15277pt]
\xleftarrow{}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\colon$}G
(or two 1-cells in a tricategory) form a
local adjunction
if there are

•

Pseudonatural transformations (2-cells)
\eta\colon\mathrm{id}\Rightarrow GF,\varepsilon\colon FG\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}

•

Modifications (3-cells)
s,t
, called
triangulators
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FGF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Rightarrow$}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\varepsilon}

satisfying the
swallowtail
equations:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFGF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}\eta_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta GF}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\varepsilon F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}tF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}Gs}

=\mathrm{id}_{\eta},

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FGFG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}{\varepsilon_{\varepsilon}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon FG}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG\varepsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}Ft}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}sG}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\eta G}

=\mathrm{id}_{\varepsilon}

This data is called a
pseudoadjunction
whenever
s
and
t
are invertible. To have triangulators satisfying these equations corresponds to having a coherent structure of adjoint functors between the hom-categories
\mathcal{D}(Fc,d)\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\xrightarrow{}\\[-2.15277pt]
\xleftarrow{}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(c,Gd)
, and clearly to have a pseudoadjunction corresponds to these adjunctions being adjoint equivalences.

3.3.2
Tensor-Hom

The special case of a pseudoadjunction defining a right closed structure on a monoidal bicategory
\mathcal{V}
have been briefly treated in Section 7.2 of
[
GS16
]
. Their version is however chosen to be
incoherent
(
s
and
t
are only required to exist). For us, a
right closed monoidal bicategory

\mathcal{V}
is one such that the pseudofunctor
-\otimes A\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
forms a pseudoadjunction
-\otimes A\dashv[A,-]
with a given pseudofunctor
[A,-]\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
for every object
A
in
\mathcal{V}
.

Remark 3.10
.

It is customary (at least in the usual 1-categorical setting) to extend this family of pseudoadjunctions to what is usually called a
parametric family
of pseudoadjunctions, meaning one for which the family of equivalences
\mathcal{V}(B\otimes A,C)\simeq\mathcal{V}(B,[A,C])
also come with the structure of a pseudonatural equivalence
\mathcal{V}(B\otimes-,C)\Rightarrow\mathcal{V}(B,[-,C])
for every pair of objects
B,C
. This depends on how the pseudofunctor
[-,-]\colon\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
is defined in its first variable on morphisms. The bicategorical Yoneda Lemma tells then how to do so, by imposing commutativity of the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}(B\otimes A^{\prime},C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}(B,[A^{\prime},C])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}(B\otimes A,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{V}(B,[A,C]).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(B\otimes f,C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

4
Building
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories

Enriched bicategories have been introduced by Garner–Shulman in
[
GS16
]
. In their setting, no use of strictification was assumed in the definition. For our purposes, it is convenient to directly consider enrichments over Gray monoids and - subsequently, for the constructions requiring a braiding - over semi-strict braided monoidal bicategories. The underlying reasoning is that these assumptions don’t really restrain the generality of our results and constructions, thanks to Theorem
2.2
.

4.1
The tricategory of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories

As proved in Section 4 of
[
GS16
]
,
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories,
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors,
\mathcal{V}
-pseudonatural transformations and
\mathcal{V}
-modifications assemble into a tricategory. Subsequently we recall terminology, structure and axioms for each kind of cell of this tricategory.

Example 4.2
.

The unit
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{I}
has one object
\ast
and the only hom-object
\mathcal{I}(\ast,\ast)=\mathbb{1}
. Since we deal in fact with a Gray monoid, multiplication and unit can be chosen to be identical, and so are
\alpha,\lambda,\rho
. This makes all coherence axioms trivially satisfied.

Remark 4.3
.

For any
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{C}
there is an associated underlying bicategory
\mathcal{C}^{\flat}
having the same objects and hom-categories
\mathcal{C}^{\flat}(c,d)\coloneqq\mathcal{V}(\mathbb{1},\mathcal{C}(c,d))
. The left and right unitors and the associator for
\mathcal{C}^{\flat}
are easily build from
\lambda,\rho
and
\alpha
respectively.

4.2

In this section we assume

Remark 4.17
.

If
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor, for
\mathcal{V}
a braided monoidal bicategory, then the opposite construction is functorial in the sense that we can induce a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor
F^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, defined by the same assignment on objects, while defining each hom-morphism just as
F^{\mathsf{op}}_{a,b}\coloneqq F_{b,a}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}(a,b)\to\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{op}}(Fa,Fb)
. The higher structure of
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor is also trivially deduced from the one of
F
, as well as the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor axioms.

4.3
The tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories

The second construction that we present is this article is that of the tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories. The definition of the structure is subsequently given.

Definition 4.18
.

Le
\mathcal{V}
be a braided monoidal bicategory (which, as before, is assumed to be semi-strict without loss of generality) and
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
be two
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories. Their product
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
is defined by the following data

•

As objects the class product
\mathcal{B}_{0}\times\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

•

For every pair of objects
(b,c),(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
, the hom-object
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}((b,c),(b^{\prime},c^{\prime}))
is given by the monoidal product
\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(c,c^{\prime})
. The composition is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{m}\colon\mathcal{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{C}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})\mathcal{C}(c,c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})\mathcal{C}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{C}(c,c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})\mathcal{C}(c,c^{\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\beta 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces mm}

and the unit of an object
(b,c)
is

\underline{u}\colon\mathbb{1}=\mathbb{1}\otimes\mathbb{1}\overset{u\otimes u}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{B}(b,b)\otimes\mathcal{C}(c,c).

•

The associator and the unitors are given by the following string diagrams:

Remark 4.19
.

In the above definition, the notation

and
\mathcal{C}
. It is worth taking a moment to translate the strings defining the structural 2-cells for the tensor product of two

Remark 4.21
.

One can easily check that the unit
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{I}
works in fact as a unit for the tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories. Moreover is it easily seen to be isomorphic to its opposite, and hence we have
\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{I}\cong\mathcal{I}
. This observation will allow us in Section
6
to talk about
constant

\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{D}
into a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{D}
.

The constant
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor over an object
d
in a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{D}
is in fact the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor
\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{D}
given by mapping the unique object
\ast
of
\mathcal{I}
over the object
d
, together with
u_{d}\colon\mathbb{1}=\mathcal{I}(\ast,\ast)\to\mathcal{D}(d,d)
.

Remark 4.22
.

It is an easy check to observe that the opposite distribute over the tensor product, in the sense that for
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
there is a
\mathcal{V}
-equivalence between
(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C})^{\mathsf{op}}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}
.

4.4
\mathcal{V}
as a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory

The present section is the last one of this article dedicated to a proof of the coherence axioms for a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory. The
\mathcal{V}
-bicategorical structure that we are going to prove is that of the monoidal bicategory
\mathcal{V}
itself, under the assumption of being right closed (Section
3.3
).

and the unit

u\colon\mathbb{1}\overset{\eta}{\longrightarrow}[a,\mathbb{1}a]=[a,a].

4.5
The enriched hom-pseudofunctor

With the ingredients of the above three sections, namely the opposite
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory, the tensor product of two
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories and the self-enrichment of
\mathcal{V}
, we are able to define hom-pseudofunctors

\mathcal{C}(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}

for every
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{C}
over a right closed braided monoidal
\mathcal{V}
. In this section we are going to provide the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor structure to
\mathcal{C}(-,-)
. To be honest, we are going to limit ourselves to providing the structure structure and the idea of a proof for
\mathcal{C}(a,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{V}
, which is defined on hom-objects as

(4.25)

\mathcal{C}(b,c)\overset{\eta}{\longrightarrow}[\mathcal{C}(a,b),\mathcal{C}(b,c)\mathcal{C}(a,b)]\overset{[1,m]}{\longrightarrow}[\mathcal{C}(a,b),\mathcal{C}(a,c)].

Then, the case
\mathcal{C}(-,b)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathcal{V}
is very similar to treat, though instances of
\beta
appear, both in the definition of the multiplication for
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}
, and in the definition of the pseudofunctor on hom-objects:

\mathcal{C}(-,d)\colon\mathcal{C}(b,c)\overset{\eta}{\longrightarrow}[\mathcal{C}(c,d),\mathcal{C}(b,c)\mathcal{C}(c,d)]\overset{[1,\beta]}{\longrightarrow}[\mathcal{C}(c,d),\mathcal{C}(c,d)\mathcal{C}(b,c)]\overset{[1,m]}{\longrightarrow}[\mathcal{C}(c,d),\mathcal{C}(b,d)].

The more general case proving that
\mathcal{C}(-,-)
is a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor is just incredibly more space-consuming, but not conceptually different, and can moreover be deduced from the existence of
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors in each variable by generalizing an easy argument which can be found at
[
EK66
]
, Chapter III, Section 4.

Theorem 4.26
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory, for
\mathcal{V}
a right closed braided monoidal bicategory. Then, for every object
a
in
\mathcal{C}
the morphism (
4.25
)
is part of a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{V}
.

5
Extra-pseudonautral transformations

In this section we introduce the enriched version of the notion of extra-pseudonatural transformation between
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
P,Q
of sort

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}.

The non-enriched case was introduced in
[
Cor16
]
. The axioms we are going to deal with are a lot, but still they are reduced in size by passing to the enriched context, just as it happens for usual pseudonatural transformations. Moreover, by keeping in mind that for every axiom (concerning
\mathcal{B}
) we have a symmetric one (concerning
\mathcal{C}
), everything reduces to keeping track of
unitality
,
functoriality
(as for pseudonatural transformations) and
compatibility
with parameters (axioms (
EU
),(
EF
),(
EC
) below). What we get for free in the enriched context is
naturality
. We specify at the outset that because of the considerable space that writing the axioms for such structures requires, we will often use intuitive (and frequent in literature) abbreviations, such as
P_{eab}
to mean
P(e,a,b)
(and the same for
Q
), or omitting to explicitly write the parameter
e
if it happens to not being crucially involved in a specific diagram.

Remark 5.1
.

It is easy to check that when a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor
P\colon\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
is defined over a tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories, we can define for every object
a
in
\mathcal{A}
and
b\in\mathcal{B}
, two
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
P_{a-}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
and
P_{-b}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
. The definition of, for example,
P_{a-}
, is clearly given by
P_{ab}
on each object
b
, and on each hom-object is the composition

P_{a-}\colon\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})\overset{u1}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{A}(a,a)\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{D}(P_{ab},P_{ab^{\prime}}).

Similarly,
P_{-b}\coloneqq P\circ 1u
. At the level of 2-cells, we have
m
PP
u1u1
P_{a-}P_{a-}
\lambda 1
uu11
1\beta 1
P
m^{\otimes}
m_{\mathcal{A}}m_{\mathcal{B}}
m_{\mathcal{A}}1
u11
u1
uu1
1m_{\mathcal{B}}
1m_{\mathcal{B}}
m_{\mathcal{B}}
1\beta 1
P_{a-}

and

u_{\mathcal{D}}
P
u_{\mathcal{A}}1
u_{\mathcal{B}}
1u_{\mathcal{B}}
u^{\otimes}
P_{a-}

where the symbols
m^{\otimes}
and
u^{\otimes}
denote the multiplication and the unit for the
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
. The proof of the axioms are an easy consequence of the corresponding axioms for
P
.

Notation 5.2
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
P\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
and

\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors, for
\mathcal{V}

from

to
Q

in
\mathcal{B}

•

Remark 5.6
.

If
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Cat}
, by whiskering the two structural 2-cells (
5.4
) and (
5.5
), with enriched morphisms
g\colon\mathbb{1}\to\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})
and
h\colon\mathbb{1}\to\mathcal{C}(c,c^{\prime})
respectively, we get the non-enriched version of the structure, that is, the 2-isomorphisms
i_{g,c}
and
i_{b,h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(e,b^{\prime},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(e,b,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q(e,c^{\prime},c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(e,b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q(e,c,c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q(e,c,c^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(e,g,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(e,g,b^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{g,c}\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{e,b,c^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{e,b,c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{b,h}\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(e,h,c^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{e,b^{\prime},c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(e,c,h)}

and the corresponding axioms which can be checked to be precisely those given in
[
Cor16
]
.

There is a notion of morphism of extra-pseudonatural transformations:

Definition 5.7
.

Let
j,j^{\prime}\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}Q
be extra-pseudonatural transformation of
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
P\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
and
Q\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
. A
morphism

\Gamma\colon j\to j^{\prime}
is the data of an indexed family of 2-morphisms in
\mathcal{V}
(for
b
in
\mathcal{B}
and
c
in
\mathcal{C}
)

\Gamma_{b,c}\colon j_{b,c}\Longrightarrow j^{\prime}_{b,c}

such that the following equalities hold true for all pairs
(b,b^{\prime})
and
(c,c^{\prime})
of objects of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
respectively:

(5.8)

This definition clearly gives rise to a category
\mathcal{V}
-
\mathsf{PsNat}^{\mathsf{e}}(P,Q)
, where composition is defined componentwise and the identity of an extra-pseudonatural transformation
j
is at each component the identical 2-cell of
j_{b,c}
in
\mathcal{V}
.

5.1
Examples

In this section we provide one main example of extra-pseudonatural transformation, which in the enriched 1-dimensional setting is the motivating example for the very introduction of the notion. First, let us recall a fact about non-enriched extra-pseudonatural transformations.

Remark 5.10
.

It is a straightforward construction that a
parametric
family of pseudoadjunctions of pseudofunctors (such as the tensor-hom, see Remark
3.10
) gives rise to extra-pseudonatural transformations. Indeed, suppose in fact we have pseudofunctors
F\colon\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
and
G\colon\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
together with, for every
e
in
\mathcal{E}
a pseudoadjunction
F(e,-)\dashv G(e,-)
, and suppose the resulting equivalences of hom-categories are part of a pseudonatural transformation

\phi\colon\mathcal{C}(F(-,d),c)\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}(d,G(-,c))

for every
c
in
\mathcal{C}
,
d
in
\mathcal{D}
. Then, the units and counits define extra-pseudonatural transformations

\eta_{d}\colon d\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}G(-,F(-,d))

and

\varepsilon_{c}\colon F(-,G(-,c))\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}c.

From the natural isomorphism
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(F(e,d),c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(c,G(e,c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(F(e^{\prime},d),c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(c,G(e^{\prime},c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(F(f,d),c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(c,G(f,c))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}\phi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{e^{\prime}}}

given by the parametric family, and since
\phi\colon g\mapsto R(c,g)\circ\eta^{c}_{d}
, we get for every
g\colon F(e,d)\to c
an isomorphism

(\phi_{f})_{g}\colon G(f,e)\circ G(e,g)\circ\eta^{c}_{d}\overset{\sim}{\Rightarrow}G(e^{\prime},g\circ F(f,d))\circ\eta^{c^{\prime}}_{d}.

Now if we take
c=F(e,d)
and
g=\mathrm{id}
, we specialize the isomorphism above to a pseudonatural transformation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(c,F(c,d))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(c^{\prime},F(c^{\prime},d))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(c^{\prime},F(c,d))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{c}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{c^{\prime}}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(f,F(c,d))}

\Leftarrow

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(c^{\prime},F(f,d))}

which is indeed the data of an extra-pseudonatural transformation
\eta_{d}\colon d\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}G(-,F(-,d))
. Then, unitality, functoriality and naturality axioms for this structure are deduced from the correspondent axioms on the pseudonatural transformation
\phi
. The argument for
\varepsilon
follows an analogous pattern.

The construction given in Remark

above will be needed in order to prove the following:

6
Enriched bi(co)ends

In this section we introduce the notion of end and coend in the enriched bicategorical context. As customary in the enriched context, we will be able to define a good notion by first defining it when valued in the ground braided monoidal bicategory
\mathcal{V}
, and then by representably extending to the general case. Our first object of interest are then
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors of type
\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}
, therefore assuming
\mathcal{V}
to be a right closed braided monoidal bicategory.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a right closed braided monoidal bicategory, and let
P\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor. An
(enriched) bicoend
of
P
is, if it exists, an object

\int^{b}P(b,b)

of
\mathcal{V}
together with an (enriched) extra-pseudonatural transformation
i\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\displaystyle\int^{b}P(b,b)
(to the constant pseudofunctor, see Remark
4.21
) such that the following two axioms hold true.

(BC1)

For any object
x
in
\mathcal{V}
and any extra-pseudonatural transformation
j\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}x
into the constant
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor over
x
, there’s a 1-cell
\tilde{j}\colon\int^{b}P(b,b)\to x
, and for every
a
in
\mathcal{B}
there is a 2-isomorphism
J_{a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int^{b}P(b,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(a,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}J_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{a}}

satisfying the following identity of 2-cells:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(a,c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(a,a)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(c,c)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),\int^{b}P(b,b)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),x]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(c,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{a}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{a}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{c}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}j_{ac}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[b]{@{}c@{}}{J_{a}}_{*}\\
\Leftarrow\end{array}}

=
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(a,c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(a,a)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(c,c)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),\int^{b}P(b,b)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),x]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(c,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{a}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{c}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}i_{ac}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}{J_{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}_{*}}

(BC2)

For every pair of 1-cells
h,k\colon\int^{b}P(b,b)\to x
and any family of 2-cells
\Gamma_{a}\colon hi_{a}\Rightarrow ki_{a}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(a,c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(a,a)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(c,c)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),\int^{b}P(b,b)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),\int^{b}P(b,b)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),x]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(c,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{a}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{a}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}i_{ac}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}{\Gamma_{a}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{*}}

=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(a,c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(a,a)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),P(c,c)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),\int^{b}P(b,b)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),\int^{b}P(b,b)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[P(c,a),x]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(c,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{a}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}i_{ac}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}{\Gamma_{c}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{*}}

there is a unique 2-cell
\gamma\colon h\Rightarrow k
such that
\Gamma_{a}=\gamma\ast i_{a}
, namely whiskering of the 2-cell
\gamma
with the 1-cell
i_{a}
.

Remark 6.2
.

One can see that an extra-pseudonatural transformation
i\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}Q
between
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors

\displaystyle P\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle Q\colon\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

consists of the same data of an extra-pseudonatural transformation
P^{\mathsf{op}}\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}Q^{\mathsf{op}}
between the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors

\displaystyle P^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes(\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{op}}

\displaystyle Q^{\mathsf{op}}\colon\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes(\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{op}}.

This is a straightforward observation based on Remarks
4.17
and
4.22
.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
P\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor. An object
\int_{b}P(b,b)
in
\mathcal{V}
together with an enriched extra-pseudonatural transformation
\int_{b}P(b,b)\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}P
defines a
biend
if the equivalent data

P^{\mathsf{op}}\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\int_{b}P(b,b)

defines a bicoend.

At this stage, the two bicoend axioms, which may look kind of obscure at first, deserve an explanation that will make them much clearer. The key point lies in the following proposition, which provides a higher point of view on bi(co)ends by defining them as representing objects.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
P\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}
be a pseudofunctor admitting a bicoend. Then, there is for every object
x
in
\mathcal{V}
an equivalence, pseudonatural in
x
, between the categories

(6.5)

\mathcal{V}(\int^{b}P(b,b),x)\simeq\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsNat}^{\mathsf{e}}(P,x).

Dually, if
P
admits a biend, there is for every
x
an equivalence

(6.6)

\mathcal{V}(x,\int_{b}P(b,b))\simeq\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsNat}^{\mathsf{e}}(x,P).

Conversely, such representing objects define bi(co)ends.

Proof.

Let us prove the first statement, and call
i\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\int^{b}P_{bb}
the bicoend of
P
. The dual statement will clearly follow a symmetric argument. The equivalence is here explicitly given as the precomposition

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\int^{b}P(b,b),x)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsNat}^{\mathsf{e}}(P,x)

defined on an object
k\colon\int^{b}P(b,b)\to x
to be the extra-pseudonatural transformation
k\circ i
with components
(k\circ i)_{b}=k\circ i_{b}
. On morphisms
\gamma\colon k\Rightarrow h
, the obvious whiskering
\gamma\ast i
, with components given by
\gamma\ast i_{b}\colon ki_{b}\Rightarrow hi_{b}
. One needs to check that
\gamma\ast i
actually defines a morphism of extra-pseudonatural transformations, which is the equality
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,Pbb]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,Pb^{\prime}b^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,x]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(b^{\prime},-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{ki_{b}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}(ki)_{bb^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{ki_{b^{\prime}}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hi_{b^{\prime}}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}{\gamma i_{b^{\prime}}}_{*}}

=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,Pbb]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,Pb^{\prime}b^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,x]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(b^{\prime},-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{ki_{b}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hi_{b}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Leftarrow$}(hi)_{bb^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hi_{b^{\prime}}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[b]{@{}c@{}}{\gamma i_{b}}_{\ast}\\
\Leftarrow\end{array}}

This is true, since expanding the composition
(ki_{b})_{\ast}
and
(hi_{b^{\prime}})\ast
we find both squares to be equal to the horizontal composition
\gamma_{*}i_{bb^{\prime}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,Pbb]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,\int^{c}Pcc]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,x]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[Pb^{\prime}b,Pb^{\prime}b^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{b}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}i_{bb^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(b^{\prime},-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{b^{\prime}}}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}\gamma_{*}}

Fully faithfulness of (
6.7
) is now precisely axiom (BC2), which states that for all families
\Gamma_{b}\colon ki_{b}\Rightarrow hi_{b}
defining a morphism of extra-pseudonatural transformations there is a unique morphism
h\Rightarrow k
in the domain category which is mapped to it by precomposition with
i
. On the other hand, axiom (BC1) says that for all objects
j\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}x
in the codomain category, there exists a
\tilde{j}\colon\int^{b}Pbb\to x
in the domain and an isomorphism of extra-pseudonatural transformations
J\colon\tilde{j}\circ i\cong j
, which is precisely essential surjectivity.
∎

6.1
Arbitrary-valued bi(co)ends

Now, the general definition of a bi(co)end valued in any
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{D}
can be given representably. As before, we suppress the parametric variable in
\mathcal{E}
for simplicity.

Definition 6.8
.

Let
P\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor, where
\mathcal{D}
is any
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory. A
biend
for
P
is an object
\int_{b}P(b,b)
in
\mathcal{D}
together with an extra-pseudonatural transformation
i\colon\int_{b}P(b,b)\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}P
such that for every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the extra-pseudonatural transformation

\mathcal{D}(d,i)\colon\mathcal{D}(d,\int_{b}P(b,b))\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\mathcal{D}(d,P(-,-))

is a biend for
\mathcal{D}(d,P(-,-))\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}
, in the sense of Definition
6.3
.

A
bicoend
for
P
is an object
\int^{b}P(b,b)
in
\mathcal{D}
together with an extra-pseudonatural transformation
i\colon P\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\int^{b}P(b,b)
such that for every object
d
in
\mathcal{D}
the extra-pseudonatural transformation

\mathcal{D}(i,d)\colon\mathcal{D}(\int^{b}P(b,b),d)\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\mathcal{D}(P(-,-),d)

is a biend for
\mathcal{D}(P(-,-),d)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}.

It immediately follows that we have a pair of equivalences in
\mathcal{V}

(6.9)

\mathcal{D}(d,\int_{b}P(b,b))\simeq\int_{b}\mathcal{D}(d,P(b,b))

(6.10)

\mathcal{D}(\int^{b}P(b,b),d)\simeq\int_{b}\mathcal{D}(P(b,b),d).

pseudonatural in
d
.

Remark 6.11
.

A natural next step would be to have the analogue result of Proposition
6.4
in the case of
\mathcal{D}
-valued bi(co)ends for an arbitrary
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory
\mathcal{D}
. In order to do so, we need to upgrade the category of bicowedges
\mathcal{V}
-
\mathsf{PsNat}^{\mathsf{e}}(P,d)
to an object in
\mathcal{V}
. In other words, we want an object
\underline{\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsFun}^{\mathsf{e}}}(P,d)
of
\mathcal{V}
giving the desired equivalence

\mathcal{D}(\int^{b}P(b,b),d)\simeq\underline{\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsNat}^{\mathsf{e}}}(P,d).

for every object (and constant pseudofunctor)
d
. The obvious choice is hence by means of (
6.10
), to define

\underline{\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsFun}^{\mathsf{e}}}(P,d)\coloneqq\int_{b}\mathcal{D}(P(b,b),d).

6.2
The enriched pseudofunctor bicategory

In the non-enriched setting it is customary and straightforward to prove that if
L,S\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
are pseudofunctors, then the biend of the pseudofunctor
\mathcal{D}(L-,S-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Cat}
exists and is given up to a canonical equivalence by
\mathsf{PsNat}(L,S)
. By virtue of this result, we can enrich the
bicategory of enriched pseudofunctors
. The following construction will provide the structure inducing unit and composition for hom-objects.

Construction 6.12
.

We subsequently explain how to construct the data for the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor enriched bicategory for two
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
. Whenever
F,G,H\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
are
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors, and if the relevant following biends exist, we look for extra-pseudonatural transformations

\underline{u}_{F}\colon\mathbb{1}\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\mathcal{D}(F-,F-)

and

\underline{m}_{F,G,H}\colon\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Gc,Hc)\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\mathcal{D}(F-,H-).

For what concerns
u_{F}
, one can define it via Proposition
5.11
to be the same data of the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudonatural
\mathrm{id}\colon F\Rightarrow F
. Hence, one has each 1-component at
d
given as

{(\underline{u}_{F})}_{d}\coloneqq u_{(Fd)}\colon\mathbb{1}\to\mathcal{D}(Fd,Fd).

Each
d
-component of
\underline{m}
is the composition

\underline{m}_{d}\colon\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Gc,Hc)\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)\overset{k_{d}\otimes j_{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{D}(Gd,Hd)\otimes\mathcal{D}(Fd,Gd)\overset{m}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd)

and 2-isomorphisms are built using the structure of extra-pseudonaturality of
k
and
j
. The 2-cell
\underline{m}_{d,d^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(d,d^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{D}(Fd^{\prime},Hd^{\prime}),\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd^{\prime})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd),\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd^{\prime})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Gc,Hc)\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc),\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd^{\prime})]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(F-,Hd^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(Fd,H-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle\Leftarrow$}\underline{m}_{dd^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{m}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{m}^{*}}

is given by the diagram
(k_{d^{\prime}}j_{d^{\prime}})^{*}
m^{\ast}
\mathcal{D}(F-,Hd^{\prime})
(k_{d}j_{d})^{\ast}
m^{\ast}
\mathcal{D}(Fd,G-)
1j_{dd^{\prime}}
k_{dd^{\prime}}1
{\underline{m}_{d^{\prime}}}^{\ast}
1j_{d^{\prime}}^{\ast}
k_{d^{\prime}}1^{\ast}
1\mathcal{D}(F-,Gd^{\prime})
1\mathcal{D}(Fd,G-)
\mathcal{D}(G-,Hd^{\prime})1
\mathcal{D}(Gd,H-)1
1j_{d^{\prime}}^{\ast}
1j_{d}^{\ast}
m_{\ast}
m_{\ast}
m_{\ast}

{\underline{m}_{d}}^{\ast}
1j_{d}^{\ast}
1j_{d}^{\ast}
k_{d^{\prime}}1^{\ast}
k_{d^{\prime}}1^{\ast}
k_{d}1^{\ast}
k_{d}1^{\ast}

The squared 2-cells come from the pseudonaturality of
m
, and can be derived from (
4.10
) (the middle one) and (
4.11
) (the side ones). The extra-pseudonatural transformation axioms then follow from the corresponding axioms for
j
and
k
.

With the previous construction we are able to enhance the structure of the bicategory
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{PsFun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
to a
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory, which we refer as
\llbracket\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\rrbracket
, in order to distinguish from the plain underlying bicategory (see Remark
4.3
).

Definition 6.13

(The
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory of
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a right closed braided monoidal bicategory and
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be two
\mathcal{V}
-bicategories, then
\llbracket\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\rrbracket
is - if the relevant biends exist - the
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory whose:

•

objects are the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctors
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D},

•

each hom-object
\llbracket\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\rrbracket(F,G)
is the object in
\mathcal{V}
defined by the biend

\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)

of the
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor
\mathcal{D}(F-,G-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{V}
.

•

The unit is defined via the extra-pseudonatural transformation
\underline{u}_{F}\colon\mathbb{1}\overset{..}{\Rightarrow}\mathcal{D}(F-,F-)
of Construction
6.12
, which provides a unique pair
(u_{F},\{U_{d}\})

(6.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Fc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(Fd,Fd)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{Fd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Rightarrow$}U_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{d}}

•

The composition is defined analogously as induced by the other extra-pseudonatural transformation
\underline{m}
of Construction
6.12
. That is,

(6.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Gc,Hc)\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Hc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\underline{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{m}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\Rightarrow$}M_{d}}

•

The left unitor
\underline{\lambda}
is defined via the equivalence (
6.6
) by defining (each component of) a morphism of extra-pseudonatural transformations (biwedges) by composing the inverses of
U
and
M
, and the unitor
\lambda
for
\mathcal{D}
as follows.

(6.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{1}\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(Fd,Fd)\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{1}\otimes\mathcal{D}(Fd,Gd)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(Fd,Gd)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Fc)\otimes\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{Fd}1}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[b]{@{}c@{}}M_{d}^{-1}\\
\Leftarrow\end{array}}

Similarly, the right unitor.

•

The associator
\underline{\alpha}
is analogously built as corresponding to a morphism of extra-pseudonatural transformations (biwedges). The latter having components as follows, where the arrows without a name are just the tensor products of the structural biend morphisms.

(6.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Gc,Hc)\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Gc)\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Ec,Fc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Gc,Hc)\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Ec,Gc)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(Fd,Hd)\mathcal{D}(Ed,Fd)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(Ed,Hd)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Fc,Hc)\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Ec,Fc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int_{c}\mathcal{D}(Ec,Hc)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\tilde{\underline{m}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\underline{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\underline{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{-45.0}{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}M_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}1M_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}M_{d}^{-1}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox[origin]{-45.0}{$\scriptstyle\Leftarrow$}M_{d}^{-1}}

The coherence identities (
IC
) and (
AC
) can then easily be proved from these definitions, and then be transposed again via the equivalence of categories (
6.6
).

7
Future perspectives

The work presented is part of my Ph.D. thesis
[
Car24
]
. In there, this bicategorical enriched machinery has been applied to the context of Mackey pseudofunctors. Mackey pseudofunctors, whose theory has been introduced in
[
BD20
]
, arise naturally in the representation theory of finite groups, and in that work from Balmer–Dell’Ambrogio it is shown that they can be seen as
\mathcal{V}
-pseudofunctor over a universal
\mathcal{V}
-bicategory for the braided monoidal bicategory
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Add}
of additive categories, additive functors and natural transformations.

In particular, all of the constructions presented in this paper and leading to the theory of bi(co)ends has been necessary in my thesis to define a Day convolution of Mackey pseudofunctor. This allows us to prove that the correct notion of a monoidal structure on such a Mackey pseudofunctor, which goes under the name of Green pseudofunctor, is precisely that of a pseudomonoid with respect to the Day convolution. A second paper about these applications will hopefully be ready soon.
